Restructured deal — MSFT keeps royalty-free frontier-model IP to 2032, **no longer pays rev-share to OpenAI**, while retaining rev-share *from* OpenAI through 2030 (margin-accretive; Q3 FY26 + JEF NDR recap, 2026-06-12). Equity-method drag: MSFT books its share of OpenAI losses in "Other, net" — $2.9B of equity-method losses 3FQ25 YTD; MS flags EPS growth accelerates in CY27 as cumulative OpenAI losses hit the cap at MSFT's initial investment (~$13B) and the drag rolls off — a tailwind not in the multiple, with MSFT at ~28x CY26 GAAP EPS (~2.0x PEG) (MS MSFT/OpenAI, 2025-05-21).
- FY27 framing: "another year of double-digit revenue and operating income growth"; headcount down y/y; opex growth mid-to-high single digits (Q3 FY26).
- **AI ARR $37B, +123% y/y**; Bernstein estimates the majority of SaaS AI ARR (~$6-7B) is M365 Copilot, with paid seats +33% q/q to 20M and >5M adds guided next quarter; flags >11% of total MSFT revenue is now growing triple digits — AI a growth driver, not a headwind (Bernstein "Cloud in the Quarter", 2026-06-22). Commercial RPO $627B, +26% ex-OpenAI / +99% incl. OpenAI's $250B+ commitment; weighted-avg duration 2.5yrs (Bernstein "Cloud in the Quarter", 2026-06-22).
- **GitHub usage exploding** — code commits ran from ~1B to ≥14B/run-rate as of Apr-2026 on AI coding tools, straining traditional CPU datacenter capacity; MSFT plans to rent AWS capacity for the GitHub CPU demand rather than divert from Azure (Bernstein "Cloud in the Quarter", 2026-06-22).
- **Funding curve — the laggard pre-funder (JPM "AI Capex 2.0", 2026-06-16):** alone among the five hyperscalers, MSFT has funded the buildout **entirely from internal cash** — **no IG bond or equity issuance in 5+ years**, a net debt repayer (~$46bn gross debt). Instead it leans on **finance leases (~$63bn at F3Q) plus ~$197bn of data-center leases not yet commenced through FY31**. JPM models capex **~$88bn FY25 → ~$140bn FY26 → ≥$240bn FY27 → ~$300bn FY28** (the $190bn CY26 outlook implies the FY27 run-rate), with the funding gap widening to ~$90bn FY27 / ~$130bn FY28 once dividends/buybacks are counted. JPM sees MSFT "~1 year behind Alphabet and Amazon on the funding curve" and points to "sizable new supply from MSFT in C2H26 and beyond" — testing AAA territory the market has long treated as risk-free (JPM "AI Capex 2.0", 2026-06-16).
- **Capex trajectory, long lens (Barron's/Serwer, 2026-05-18):** **$24B (FY2021) → $88B (FY2025) → $190B projected (CY2026 basis)** — the FY2025 $88B figure lines up with JPM's own FY25 capex estimate (see the funding-curve item below), corroborating the scale of the ramp from an independent generalist source; the $190B figure is on a CY2026 basis (vs JPM's FY-basis progression to ≥$240B FY27).
- **Token-budget exhaustion (JPM "AI Capex 2.0", 2026-06-16):** JPM cites Microsoft (with Uber) as an enterprise that **exhausted its full-year 2026 token budget within months** as agents entered production — a demand-side datapoint for agentic-inference compute intensity. Maia 200 reiterated at **~30% better perf/$** vs the latest hardware in MSFT's own fleet.
- **Barron's "Top CEOs of 2026" profile of Satya Nadella (Jack Hough, 2026-06-22):** generalist framing of the AI-transition thesis (OpenAI stake, Copilot) — **"the stock is up tenfold since then, for a 23% annual total return,"** with MSFT **"growing sales 18% in the past 12 months and earnings per share by 30%."** Corroborates the sales/EPS growth already on file (Q3 FY26 rev +18%, EPS +21% ex-OpenAI) from a trailing-12-month generalist lens.
- **Layoffs ~4,800 (~2%), gaming unit cut ~20% (The Information, 2026-07-06, "The Briefing: Microsoft's Cuts"):** MSFT is laying off **~4,800 people (~2% of workforce)**, mostly in **sales and the Xbox/gaming business**, and selling **4-5 small gaming studios**. Including ~1,600 more gaming cuts over the next year, MSFT is **cutting ~20% of its gaming unit**. New gaming chief **Asha Sharma** told staff "our business today is not healthy." A cost-discipline / margin-defense datapoint (consistent with the flat-FY27-op-margin commitment and the Xbox price hikes already on file) but also a candid admission of gaming weakness within More Personal Computing (The Information, 2026-07-06).

- **The AMD relationship has history, and the new Helios commitment is best read as OpenAI capacity, not fleet diversification (SemiAnalysis "Can AMD break the CUDA Moat? AMD Advancing AI 2026", 2026-07-24):** the unflattering-but-relevant backdrop to the 07-20 "MSFT formalizes its AMD Helios commitment" datapoint already on this page. Per SemiAnalysis: "**In 2023, Microsoft dropped AMD as a customer after MI300X due to unreliable Samsung 2023 HBM memory & poor software quality and skipped both MI325X and MI355X.**" On the new announcement: "Now Microsoft has announced that it will deploy MI455X Helios, **we believe that this will be mainly for OpenAI as the end customer for Azure's MI455X racks**." That materially reframes the diversification read — the Azure AMD commitment looks like **capacity procured for a single anchor tenant ([[OPENAI]])**, not a broad second-sourcing of the Azure fleet, which is consistent with (a) the 07-13 Archera expert call already on this page (Azure "worst of the three" on third-party inference because it has **only Nvidia GPUs and no custom ASIC to offload internal demand**), and (b) JPM Schilsky's 07-23 "No Model, No Chip, No Hope?" bear framing. Also from the same note: AMD is pairing with **Cerebras** for PD-disaggregated ultra-fast interactivity inference ("a strategy somewhat similar to NVIDIA Groq"). Note this is SemiAnalysis's belief, not an MSFT or AMD disclosure. (SemiAnalysis, 2026-07-24) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_24_ENC_Can_AMD_break_the_CUDA_Moat_AMD_Advancing_AI_2026_email.html)
- **🆕 READ-THROUGH — AMD has now put the Azure Helios commitment on the record on an earnings call, and its framing is BROADER than the "really just OpenAI" read directly above (AMD Q2 2026 earnings call, Bloomberg LIVE TRANSCRIPT, 2026-08-04).** The 07-20 announcement is already on this page; what is net-new is that **Lisa Su named both the workload and the customer scope in prepared remarks**: _"We also expanded our long standing partnership with Microsoft. **Microsoft will deploy Helios at scale on Azure for FRONTIER MODEL INFERENCING across Microsoft, its AI customers, and Azure AI services**."_ Two specifics the announcement did not carry: **(1) the workload is INFERENCING, not training** — consistent with AMD positioning Helios as an inference rack rather than a training platform; **(2) the customer scope is three-part — Microsoft itself, its AI customers, and Azure AI services.** ⚠️ **That does NOT reconcile with SemiAnalysis's 07-24 read one bullet above ("we believe that this will be mainly for OpenAI as the end customer for Azure's MI455X racks"). AMD's on-record framing is a broad Azure-fleet deployment; SemiAnalysis's is single-tenant capacity procurement for [[OPENAI]]. Both stay on the page: the vendor has an incentive to describe the widest possible scope, and SemiAnalysis has been right on this relationship before. The arbitrating observable is whether Azure exposes MI455X instance types to third-party customers, or only runs them behind first-party/OpenAI endpoints.** One further clause from the same remarks, on the CPU side rather than the accelerator side: Su listed **Microsoft among the hyperscalers "expanding EPYC across their internal infrastructure and public cloud offerings" (with AWS, Google and Oracle)**, and disclosed that **fifth-gen EPYC Turin "now powers nearly one third of the more than 1,600 EPYC public cloud instance types available globally"** — a scale mark on the x86 second-source that is relevant to Azure's cost-per-VM, not to the AI story. See [[AMD]].
- **🆕 READ-THROUGH — the price MSFT paid for hosted capacity now looks well-timed: NVDA is paying the SAME counterparty ~50% more per MW (Bernstein · Gautam Chhugani, "AI Infra/miners: Texas data center audit to drive more MW scarcity", 2026-08-04).** Bernstein's Exhibit 2 puts IREN's **revenue yield per IT MW up ~50%, from ~$10mn under the Microsoft contract to ~$15mn under the NVIDIA contract**; on a **gross-MW** basis the same series reads **~$11mn (MSFT) → ~$14mn (NVDA) → ~$11mn (Enterprises)**. ⚠️ **The two bases are different series and must not be mixed or averaged — the ~50% step-up is the IT-MW series; the gross-MW series steps up ~27% and then falls back for enterprises.** Bernstein's own logic: _"If MWs are scarce, it makes sense to earn more per MW"_ — with **recent enterprise cloud deals indicating GPU-hour rates 20-25% higher than previous guidance** and IREN **targeting $4bn ARR on 480 MW of cloud deployment**. **The read for MSFT is the mirror image of the [[NVDA]] read on the same exhibit: the Iren contract already on this page (Cantor 06-18, ~$10B over ~5yrs, the Childress/Sweetwater anchor) was struck at roughly two-thirds of the current clearing price per IT MW — i.e. MSFT locked in cheap hosted capacity ahead of the re-rate, and any RENEWAL of that block should be underwritten at the new level, not the old one.** ⚠️ **Note this is a counterparty's realised revenue yield, not a disclosed MSFT contract price — it is the best available proxy, not a Microsoft number.** IREN is not a wiki-covered name. Scarcity mechanism in the bullet below · [themes/ai-datacenter-power](themes/ai-datacenter-power.md).
- **🆕 READ-THROUGH — the mechanism behind the re-rate: Texas has PAUSED its data-center interconnection review (Bernstein · Gautam Chhugani, 2026-08-04).** Governor Greg Abbott has directed the **PUCT and ERCOT to audit ALL data-center projects in ERCOT's interconnection process**, and **ERCOT has paused the "Batch Zero" review that was due to publish classification results on 2026-08-07**. Context figure: the **ERCOT interconnection queue stands at 474 GW of REQUESTS, with data centers ~90% of new requests** — ⚠️ **a QUEUED/REQUESTED number, not energised, not IT-load, not facility GW; it is not comparable to any of the GW figures elsewhere on this page (see `_meta/assumptions.md` — never mix GW bases).** Bernstein's conclusion is the part that matters for a hyperscaler with signed capacity: the audit **"throttles speculative data center pipeline and makes genuine sites with development history more valuable"** — i.e. **already-approved MW re-rate upward and time-to-power lengthens for everything not yet approved.** For MSFT that cuts both ways: it supports the value of contracted/approved capacity already on the books (and the +1GW/quarter cadence management reported at the print), and it lengthens the runway for the incremental Texas capacity behind the FY27-28 build. Subject of the note is the bitcoin-miner / AI-infra cohort, not Microsoft. See [themes/ai-datacenter-power](themes/ai-datacenter-power.md).
- **MSFT as a hyperscaler demand input in BofA's power-semis model (BofA "Watts to Tokens", Vivek Arya, 2026-05-25, read-through):** MSFT's Azure AI buildout is one of the hyperscaler demand inputs behind BofA's bottoms-up rack/accelerator model — rising compute density drives rack power ~100x to ~1.5MW at Nvidia's Feynman platform (CY29/30), with ~233GW of accelerator capacity deployed through CY30 expanding the AI analog-semi market to $27bn (28% CAGR) inside a $1.7tn AI-DC-systems TAM. Microsoft's own **Maia** custom ASIC appears in BofA's per-platform table at ~0GW cumulative (negligible ramp vs Google TPU / AWS Trainium) — consistent with the wiki's read that Maia remains the laggard of the hyperscaler ASIC programs. Light read-through (MSFT is a demand driver, not the subject); see [ai-datacenter-power](themes/ai-datacenter-power.md) / [custom-asic-tpu](themes/custom-asic-tpu.md). [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Bofa_Watt_to_tokens.html)
- **Fairwater Atlanta (JPM "SpaceX — The Next Frontier", 2026-07-07, read-through):** JPM's SpaceX initiation names **Microsoft's Fairwater Atlanta among the largest AI facilities** it benchmarks the SpaceX/COLOSSUS build against — a passing mention (no MSFT-specific numbers), corroborating the multi-GW Fairwater campus scale already tracked on this page (Fairwater Wisconsin ~2GW+, SemiAnalysis 2025-11-12). Link-only; see [SPCX.md](SPCX.md).
- **Q3 FY26 print recaps + IR call (UBS/Citi/MSFT IR, Apr–May 2026):** Azure +39% (Mar) with the June guide to +40% high-end and a "modest" 2H acceleration framed as coming from **efficiency gains, not capacity adds** (40% more token throughput on the same hardware; doc-to-live times down ~30–40%) — UBS models 42–43% exiting the year (UBS/Keirstead, 2026-04-30). Capacity is allocated weekly across **three buckets — Azure platform / 1P apps (M365 + GitHub Copilot) / internal R&D** — and MSFT added ~1GW in each of FQ2 and FQ3, targeting ~doubling the fleet in 2 years; Fairwater scales to "a couple GW" over time (MSFT IR call, 2026-05-18). The OpenAI rev-share removal lands in **COGS, not Azure contra-revenue** (so the Azure acceleration is fully organic), the **AGI clause was removed** ("a very big win" — adds visibility), and OpenAI API exclusivity loss is downplayed given MSFT is capacity-constrained anyway — corroborated by Oracle now being able to resell OpenAI APIs on OCI for the first time (UBS/Citi recaps, 2026-04-30; WFC ORCL recap, 2026-06-11). Citi called the print a "clearing event" (Azure clarity + PC/Windows-OEM weakness "ripped off") setting up a positive revision story (Citi, 2026-04-30). [UBS](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_04_30_msft_ubs_recap_30_par_2026.html) · [Citi](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_04_30_msft_wfc_c1q26_recap_30_apr_26.html) · [MSFT IR](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_05_18_msft_ir_call_18_may_26.html) · [ORCL recap](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_06_11_orcl_wfc_recap_11_jun_26.html)
- **Capex ramp / FCF (UBS, 2026-04-30):** CY26 capex guided $190B ($40B June qtr, ~$60B/qtr in 2H); annualizing implies **FY27 ~$240B (~+65%)** — UBS raised its FY27 capex est from ~$181B to ~$234B, pushing modeled FY27 **FCF to ~−$21B**. June-qtr gross margin guided to mid-66% (down ~220bps y/y) even after the OpenAI rev-share removal helps; Dynamics 365 renewal weakness (17%→low-double-digit guide) flagged as a negative SaaS read; commercial bookings +7% ex-OpenAI, RPO ~flat (UBS/Keirstead, 2026-04-30). MSFT says there is **no FCF floor** — capex is demand-signal-driven — while staying committed to flat FY27 operating margins (Citi, 2026-04-30).
- **Neocloud capacity sourcing — scale + counterparty color (Cantor neocloud desk, 2026-06-18):** MSFT has signed **11 separate deals with CoreWeave** and keeps renewing/adding, validating the "rent-and-resell" leg of Azure capacity; on contract-renewal mechanics, Cantor expects MSFT to re-sign expiring GPU blocks (e.g. a contracted 100k GB300s) at the then-prevailing spot, not walk. Among the crypto-miner→neocloud converters, the **MSFT contract is the anchor for Iren's "Childress/Sweetwater" campuses (Horizon 1–4)** — Cantor pegs it at **~$10B over ~5yrs** and frames it as the credibility validator the whole neocloud cohort needed (Cantor neocloud desk, 2026-06-18). Read-through: MSFT's neocloud reliance is broad (CoreWeave + Iren + ORCL) and ongoing, reinforcing the gross-basis Azure-margin-dilution point already on file.
- **Azure enterprise-contract terms tightened (Archera AI-compute expert, 2026-04-09):** as of **Mar 1 2026** MSFT restructured the Azure committed-consumption (MACC) agreement — deals signed before are grandfathered, but **new contracts carry an annual spend target that escalates yearly, with a cash true-up if missed** (vs the old "spend the pool anytime over 3 years"). The expert also flags Azure finally able to **serve Claude tokens** but with smaller code-gen wins than Google, and that among digital-natives/ISVs adding a *second* production cloud, **MSFT is winning ~1/3 vs Google ~2/3** — Google having surpassed Azure as the single biggest token provider to this expert's book (Archera, 2026-04-09). A demand-side nuance to the multi-model/Azure-acceleration story.
- **Azure the "worst of the three" on inference capacity, but first to Rubin (Archera expert call, 2026-07-13):** a follow-up from the same Archera AI-compute expert franchise — Azure is judged **the most capacity-constrained hyperscaler for third-party inference** because it has **only Nvidia GPUs (no custom ASIC to offload internal demand)** and prioritizes its scarce inference capacity to internal SKUs (GitHub Copilot, Azure OpenAI Foundry). The knock-on: **managed-services SKUs (Foundry/Azure OpenAI) enjoy a capacity edge over bare-metal**, and MSFT couldn't bring on marginal A100/H100/H200 blocks for external customers the way AWS/Google did. Strategically, Azure has **focused on deploying leading-edge silicon (Blackwell, now Rubin) on long-term contracts at attractive rates** rather than re-pricing lagging-edge inference capacity, and the expert expects **Azure (with some neoclouds) to be the first to deploy Rubin in earnest in 2H** via big forward "Max Lift" committed-consumption commitments. Reiterates the Mar-1-2026 MACC restructuring (annual spend targets + cash true-up) already documented from the 04-09 Archera call. [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_13_archera_13_jul_26.html)
- **GitHub Copilot strain corroborated (Allen Roush AI-coding expert, 2026-04-28):** independent confirmation that **GitHub is suffering material downtime tied to the Copilot deployment** (availability "not even one nine" vs the historical 3–5 nines), echoing the CPU-capacity strain MSFT itself flagged; the expert separately validates the Copilot multi-model toggle (Claude/OpenAI/auto inside one app) as real and underrated, and notes **Accenture rolled Copilot to >700k users** (defending the seat-momentum bull case against "Copilot sucks" skeptics) (Allen Roush, 2026-04-28).
- **UBS marks its own model to the memory cycle two days before the print — PT $510 → $480, FY27 capex $234B → $261B, FY27 M365 growth +70bps (UBS · Karl Keirstead / Taylor McGinnis / Jack Fyda, "Update on Azure, Capex and M365," 2026-07-26):** a standalone note built on "a dozen or so recent conversations with Microsoft customers and partners," and the first place UBS puts hard numbers behind the 07-25 "Tech Musings" preview already on this page. **Buy retained, PT cut $510 → $480** on a **CY27 non-GAAP P/E of 21x (prior 22x)** — "given weaker AI infrastructure market sentiment," explicitly a multiple call, not an estimate cut. The two model changes pull in opposite directions and that is the point ("more tactically balanced into this print"): **FY27 capex $234B → $261B** on merchant-TPU/memory inflation, while **FY27 M365 Commercial Cloud growth goes +70bps to 16.2%** on Copilot/Cowork improvements plus "the confluence of M365 segment price changes." The set-up numbers worth carrying: the stock is **-21% YTD**; UBS estimates the **investor bogey for FY27 capex is already $255-260B** (so UBS's own new $261B is now *at* the bogey rather than below it — a material change vs the $234B mark that sat ~$25B under it); valuation **19x CY27E GAAP / 17x non-GAAP**. The explicit conclusion on the set-up: because "many investors are already mixed about the stock and the AI infrastructure market in general," the positioning "mitigates the downside risk **even if AI capex were to rise and the 2H Azure guide was reaffirmed (not raised)**" — i.e. UBS is pre-committing to a benign read of the exact outcome the bears expect. What UBS is explicitly *not* underwriting: "capacity constraints on external customers and early token optimization efforts **could limit the potential for material Azure guidance upside**," with the Street "not rewarding the stocks of AI infra providers that signal higher capex spend and/or don't post upside to cloud revs growth and margins (**see Oracle and Google**)" — the read-across from the [[GOOG]] print made explicit. One monetization mechanic new to the page: the **GitHub Copilot pricing change offers "a modest Azure growth rate boost."** (UBS, 2026-07-26)
- **The Evidence Lab field checks behind the 07-26 UBS note — ~a dozen customer/partner conversations, and the verbatims are the differentiated part (UBS · Karl Keirstead / Taylor McGinnis / Jack Fyda "Microsoft Corp. — Update on Azure, Capex and M365", 2026-07-26):** the bullet above carries the model changes; this is the primary-evidence half, none of which was previously on this page. **(1) Azure capacity constraints are now hitting EXTERNAL customers, and UBS flags it as new:** "If there was one thing that bubbled up in these checks that sounded new, it was the few references above to customers experiencing Azure capacity constraints. This is 'new' because in most of our conversations and survey work over the last 1-2 years, this constraint hasn't come up much." Partner 6: "**We are still seeing Microsoft being very capacity constrained but it's the worst for companies that are all-in on Microsoft. They are basically being told that there is no capacity in certain regions or it is 20% higher cost.** We are telling customers it's safer to just sign pay as you go contracts." Partner 2 is blunter on the allocation question: "Azure slowness has been more due to capacity restraints that Microsoft has. We've had customers say they are trying to expand and get pushback from Microsoft. These are F500 customers trying to increase their Azure environment. **What it seems like to me — Microsoft hasn't said this — is that they're reserving capacity for AI rather than enterprise customers.**" Same partner, on the end-June business review: "They told us Azure growth hasn't been as strong with us and we said there was capacity constraints for customers. They said they knew that and they were working hard to build more data centers… that could be months and months because data centers don't pop up overnight. **We're not tweaking our 2H Azure targets. We're really pushing Google to diversify against Microsoft. Microsoft made some painful changes to us on EA rebates that lost us pure margin.**" UBS is explicit it can't source the cause — "whether Microsoft simply didn't add as much capacity as it had in the previous quarters (**1 GW per quarter**) or because Microsoft allocated more of its incremental capacity to internal needs" — and notes the contradiction with the Build-conference claim that MSFT "had added more capacity in the last 18 months than it had in the previous prior decade." **The cross-check cuts against the checks:** UBS's own semi-annual Enterprise AI survey (~130 enterprises, Mar-26) has **"material" GPU/capacity constraints flagged by <10% of respondents** (UBS's own words; the Mar-26 vs Oct-25 series split in the chart is not legible in the extract, so only the <10% figure is carried), so this is a Microsoft-specific, not industry-wide, signal. Directly corroborates the 07-27 Business Insider "compute crunch forces MSFT to prioritise its own AI products over Azure cloud customers" row already on this page, and the 07-13 Archera "Azure worst of the three on third-party inference" read. **(2) Token optimization is real but early, and the down-tiering is away from frontier:** "the majority of our enterprise customer checks are now signaling plans to reign-in this growth and optimize token spend, in part by down-tiering to lower-cost AI models," with "no strong consensus yet" on the near-term hyperscaler revenue impact. Customer 2 supplies the sharpest cost curve on this page: "**My spend per developer used to be $100. Then it went to $200. Now I got an average of $1,500 a person. And that's within 6-7 months. We went from spending $23,000 to $100,000 a month**… You don't need the state of the art models all the time. **I don't need Opus 4.8 to do all the work. I can go down to maybe OpenAI Mini for 90% of what I do.** Model routing is mainly manual." Customer 8: "We got our first bill and leadership said '**we don't have the money for this**'." Customer 9 is the multi-model defection datapoint: "**We are diversifying our AI partners, moving away from our Microsoft centricity, really just for risk management… we are now talking directly with Anthropic and OpenAI**" — while funding token growth by "tempering headcount growth" and "pulling back on our spending growth with external IT services partners" rather than cutting AI. **(3) GitHub Copilot's June 1 credit-based repricing is a bigger revenue event than modelled:** "a large bank customer of GitHub Copilot told us that their **GitHub Copilot spend was up 10-20x (yes, 10-20 times and not %) post the pricing change**"; Customer 6: "With consumption-based billing, it was much more expensive. If you had 100 employee seats, maybe 60 were using it a ton and 40 were not… **with consumption, maybe those 60 are generating a bill that was the same as 150 employee seat licenses**." Offsetting churn risk is named (April GitHub outages lingering into June; GitLab low-end displacements; Cursor launching a Git-repo alternative), and Partner 2 is the hard negative: "**Over the years we've had customers move away from GitHub Copilot. I don't think it's been competitive… performance is bad with the code you get. I talk to a lot of developers who say it's the worst. They don't use it. It's mostly Claude Code and Codex.**" **(4) M365 Copilot — steady, not inflecting, and the partner quota data is ugly:** Partner 1 (bull) — "it's definitely not the same product it was on launch day… **it's close to doubling the number of seats added in the FY ended June**. Also in usage and adoption, that's increasing 60-70%," and "Microsoft expects **6x growth in Copilot seats for SME&C next year**." Partner 2 (bear) — "**We achieved 14% of Microsoft's Copilot plan. If they wanted us to sell 100 seats, we only sold 14**… We were **#1 in goal attainment** and we have **347% y/y growth** on Copilot. To their goal, we were still very low. So the rest of the partners missed more. **It's too expensive. $30/user/month is a lot. If it was $10-$15, it would be more in expectations**… it's unfortunate because Microsoft owns PowerPoint and Word, but **Claude and ChatGPT create better PowerPoints and results**." Partner 7 reframes the driver entirely: "**The growth driver for M365 is not actually Copilot. The main one is the Microsoft strategy of gradually moving customers from E3 to E5**… **regular Copilot is stale**." **(5) Cowork: constructive but too early, and the consumption charge is the friction.** Partner 1 priced an actual task — "I pointed it at a library of 10-15 folders… **It would have cost 6,500 credits, or $65.** If you think that saved 25 hours of work, then that's a great deal but **it can spin up fast**." Partner 7: "**The consumption piece is a non-starter. On top of the fixed $30 fee, customers don't see the value.**" Customer 6: "the existing capability in Copilot is **good enough to do 60-70% of what Cowork offers**." Partner 6 states the P&L mechanic cleanly: "**You buy Copilot in M365 but anything agentic you are spinning tokens in Azure**" (Partner 1: "With Cowork, consumption credits will show up in Azure consumption"). **(6) E7 is being bought on discount, so the ARPU uplift is minimal so far:** "three partners noted Microsoft is motivating initial E7 upgrades via discounts, such that the uplift vs E5 is likely minimal to date." Partner 1: "We've been seeing customers buying E7, not wall to wall yet but **10-50% across the user base in return for anywhere between a 10-50% discount. If you get a 50% discount, it's cheaper than E5.**" Partner 2: "We're closing a **2,800 seat deal on E7. It's $99 right now. That includes the discount. We're told that there will be a 15% increase after the initial promo period.**" Partner 6 lays out the renewal squeeze: "They were paying **$38 per user per month for E5 but on renewal that is going to be $60** because they won't give any discount with E5. **Or you can go to E7 for the low price of $70**, so each cycle your leverage gets less and less." Partner 7: "Microsoft is strategically and perhaps intentionally **limiting the E7 upgrade to a few customers. It's almost a selective invite**… In general, customers are not asking about E7." Net read: the field work is materially more cautious on Azure capacity and Copilot quota attainment than the sell-side marks on this page, and materially more bullish on GitHub Copilot consumption revenue and M365 list-price capture than either. [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/ued48088.html)
- **The full UBS estimate set behind the 07-26 PT cut — and the capex number is ALL-IN, not cash (UBS · Karl Keirstead / Taylor McGinnis / Jack Fyda "Microsoft Corp. — Update on Azure, Capex and M365", 2026-07-26):** the page carries the PT and the $261B headline; these are the underlying marks, all net-new. **EPS (UBS diluted): 06/26E $16.95 unchanged (cons $16.80), with Q4E $4.41 vs cons $4.24; 06/27E CUT to $19.26 from $19.49 (cons $19.40); 06/28E CUT to $22.12 from $22.68 (cons $22.78)** — i.e. UBS is *above* consensus on the June quarter about to print and *below* it two years out, which is the whole "tactically balanced" posture in two numbers. Revenue **$330,768m / $388,382m / $457,952m (06/26E-06/28E)**; **EBIT margin 46.9% → 46.2% → 45.5%**; **equity FCF yield collapses to 0.1% in 06/27E** (from 2.4% in 06/26E, recovering to 1.5%/2.7% in 06/28E-06/29E); balance sheet flips from **net cash $51,414m (06/25) → $49,906m (06/26E) → $1,299m (06/27E) → net debt $(13,408)m (06/28E)** — the arithmetic behind JPM's credit downgrade and the "first debt issuance in a decade" call already on this page. **The capex definition matters and resolves a spread on this page:** UBS's Figure 4 splits FY27E all-in capex of **$260,593m into $209,014m of purchased property & equipment plus $51,579m acquired under capital leases** (FY26E: $146,149m = $112,201m + $33,948m). So the $261B is an **all-in, lease-inclusive** number — not directly comparable to BofA's $243.5B buy-side bogey or the Street's $230-235B sell-side consensus, and the reason UBS can raise capex ~$25B while modelling FY27 **FCF (OCF less *cash* capex) at ~break-even +$3B** rather than the ~-$21B it carried in April. Near-term capex also nudged up: **4Q/Jun to $41.8b from $41.0b**, and **CY2026 to $202b vs the $190b guide**. **Equity raise: low probability** — "we can envision Microsoft accessing the debt financing markets, but we are attaching a low probability to the company turning to the equity markets"; the reason is price, not need — "Google did so at what was an all-time share price high. **Microsoft shares are almost 30% off its highs**… Microsoft's CFO will not be eager to issue equity at these levels." **Azure estimates deliberately UNCHANGED** despite everything above: 4Q/Jun guide 39-40% cc with "the investor consensus… for a 1-point beat to 41%, in-line with our estimate," and UBS still models **42-43% by 2Q/Dec 2026** — but flags the arithmetic that caps the bull case: **for growth to reach the mid-40s in 2HC26, sequential dollar adds in 1Q/Sep and 2Q/Dec would need to reach nearly $3bn, vs UBS's 4Q/Jun estimate of $3.4bn**. **GitHub Copilot revenue raised** on the June 1 repricing: **4Q/Jun $289m → $326m; FY27 $1.57b → $1.95b, adding ~40bps to FY27 Azure growth.** **Margins:** 3Q/Mar GM was down only **110bps y/y despite $37bn (11% of revs) of AI revenue** — UBS's evidence that "the magnitude of both GM and OM pressure… has not proven to be as material as investors feared 1-2 years ago," and "we've made the same observation about the margin performance at **AWS, Oracle** and others — on the surface, AI capex/revs are not landing at the abysmal margin levels that the bears feared" (a direct contest of the Redburn ~12-13% Gen-AI-margin thesis already on this page). Forward: 4Q/Jun GM guided **66.2%** vs UBS's prior 67.7% (**-240bps y/y**), 4Q OM guide **44.0%** (~130bps below UBS), **Cloud GM 64% in 4Q/Jun from 66% in 3Q/Mar**; FY27 modelled at **GM -250bps to 65.5% and OM -70bps to 46.2%**, with UBS explicitly **declining to cut FY27 GM/OM despite raising capex** because "Microsoft appears to be aggressively exercising levers." UBS's guidance call: "we would not be surprised to hear Microsoft set its initial **FY27 EBIT margin guide to be 'down modestly' (perhaps by 50-100bps)**," which investors will read as flat-with-upside — "an outcome that in our view is in-line, not negative." **On the OpenAI accounting change, the sign is the opposite of the intuitive read:** "the revs share that Microsoft was previously paying to OpenAI **landed in COGS (it was not a contra-revs item)** and the removal of this revs share under the terms of the new OpenAI agreement therefore **boosts GMs beginning in 4Q/Jun**" — i.e. the guided 66.2% GM is *after* that tailwind, making the underlying compression worse than it looks. **M365, quantified:** the segment is **~$94bn**, grew **15% in 3Q/Mar** (1pt above the 13-14% guide) on **stable 6% seat growth** ("no evidence of Office seat growth compression"), guided to **13-14% cc in 4Q/Jun (15-16% adjusted for prior-period items)**; UBS models **16% cc in 1Q/Sept and beyond**, the high end of the 14-16% band MSFT has posted for **eight straight quarters**. The price-change stack driving it: **EA discount elimination effective Nov 1 2025** (partner estimates of a **5-15% net price increase**), the **M365/O365 list increase effective Jul 1 2026** (partners report a **7-10% effective cost increase**), and **Cowork consumption billing on top of the seat** (confirmed by MSFT as reflected in the 4Q/Jun guidance). UBS's bottoms-up on the July 1 change alone (Figure 5): **468.4m total O365 paid seats**, **13.8% weighted-average price increase**, **33% of the base renewing in FY27** with a 50% rev-rec timing adjustment → **$1,412m of FY27 contribution = 227bps of O365 Commercial growth, 160bps of FY27 M365 Commercial Cloud growth, and 43bps of total MSFT revenue growth** (the body text rounds this to "~150bps"; the fall-2025 EA change is *additional* and unmodelled). One partner's summary of the licensing regime: "**Microsoft has made more changes to its licensing in the last year than it has in the last 10 years.**" [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/ued48088.html)
- **Maia finally shows up in the TSMC CoWoS allocation table — small, but net-new (Fubon "Our advanced packaging, CoWoS forecast adjustments; other updates", Sherman Shang / Daniel Yang, 2026-07-28):** in Fubon's TSMC CoWoS customer-mix forecast, **MSFT enters the mix for the first time — 0.0% (2023/24/25) → 1.3% (2026) → 1.0% (2027)**, i.e. **0 → 15,000 → 18,900 wafers** against a total pool going **675,000 (2025) → 1,155,600 shipped / 1,170,000 capacity (2026) → 1,890,000 (2027)**. For scale, the same table has **NVDA 50.1%/50.0% (586,500 → 945,000 wafers), AVGO 22.5%/22.3% (263,100 → 421,800), AWS 10.9%/10.4% (127,500 → 196,500), AMD 7.2%/7.0%, MediaTek 1.5% → 7.3%** — so Microsoft is the smallest named allocation in the book. ⚠️ **This nuances, without overturning, the "dead last on custom ASICs" read already on this page:** BofA's 05-25 per-platform table put Maia at ~0GW cumulative through CY30 and SemiAnalysis (2025-11-12) called Maia 200 an internal failure with CoWoS bookings far below GOOG/AMZN/Meta — Fubon's forecast is consistent with "far below peers" but is the first datapoint showing a **non-zero, growing 2026-27 CoWoS booking** for Microsoft silicon. **+26% wafers in 2027 on a pool growing +64%** is a share *decline* (1.3% → 1.0%) with absolute volume still rising — the same denominator effect Fubon shows for AWS. Note Fubon does not name the part; Maia is the inference from what is already on this page, not a Fubon disclosure. (Fubon "Our advanced packaging, CoWoS forecast adjustments; other updates", 2026-07-28) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Our_advanced_packaging_CoWoS_forecast_adjustments_other_up.html)
- **FY26 10-K — the hard actuals under the capex debate (filed with the 4QFY26 print, 2026-07-29, accession 0001193125-26-323660):** FY26 (ended Jun-30-26) **cash capex $115.9B, +80% y/y** (FY25 $64.6B), plus **$24.6B of finance-lease ROU assets obtained** (FY25 $20.5B) → **all-in FY26 ≈ $140.6B on a cash + finance-lease basis** (computed from the two 10-K lines). ⚠️ CORRECTED 07-30 (post-audit): quarterly on that same basis, all filing-sourced, the series is **$28.5B / $36.2B / $34.9B / $40.9B** (Q4 cash capex $35.8B — confirmed by the print) — the earlier "$42.7B implied Q4" mixed this basis with MSFT's reported quarterly spend figures ($37.5B/$31.9B) and is retracted. Do NOT read $140.6B against JPM's ~$140B FY26 model: JPM's series is on the reported basis (~$88B FY25), a different measure — the match is a coincidence of incompatible bases. **On an accrual basis FY26 incurred capex is ≈$160B**: the 10-K shows **$26.7B of capex sitting in accounts payable** at Jun-30 vs $6.9B a year ago — a +$19.8B accrual/cash wedge (10-K, property-and-equipment note). The forward pipeline is the headline: **datacenter leases not yet commenced $329.1B — 3.55x the FY25 10-K's $92.7B**, with the full primary trail (⚠️ corrected 07-30 to filings, replacing the JPM secondary): $92.7B (Jun-25) → $106.2B (Sep-25) → $155.1B (Dec-25) → $196.6B (Mar-26) → $329.1B (Jun-26) — **+$132.5B landed in the June quarter alone**; commencement window extended to **FY27–FY33**, and with **new conditionality language — "with some arrangements subject to certain contractual conditions being met"** — absent from the Q1 10-Q wording. ⚠️ CORRECTED 07-30 (post-audit): the investing "Other, net" outflow ($(19.9)B FY26 vs +$2.3B *inflow* FY25; MD&A: "$22.2 billion increase… primarily to facilitate the purchase of components") funds **"other receivables related to activities to facilitate the purchase of server components" — a RECEIVABLE MSFT expects repaid, not a prepayment: $27.8B at Jun-26 vs $8.2B at Jun-25** (the program predates FY26 — $10.5B at Jun-24). The filing says "server components," never "memory" — the memory link is this page's inference via the ~$25B memory chunk in the $190B CY26 guide, not a disclosure. Two liquidity qualifiers alongside: **$11.3B of investments restricted pursuant to a supplier agreement** ($3.8B in STI + $7.5B in equity/other — qualifies the $76.8B cash+STI figure) and **$34.6B committed for construction** (vs $32.1B at FY25). Funding: **zero debt issuance in FY26** ($3.0B repaid; cash+STI down $94.6B → $76.8B; OCF $182.9B, +34%) — but the 10-K carries **an entirely new cloud/AI-investment risk factor — zero hits for its language in the FY26 Q1 10-Q (which restates Item 1A in full) *or* the FY25 10-K** (audit-verified 07-30; the claim is stronger than first logged): investments "are being made at significant scale and on an accelerated timeline, **require substantial and increasing capital expenditures and continued access to capital**, and are **in advance of fully developed revenue streams**," plus an explicit sentence on interest rates/credit markets/credit ratings limiting "our ability to execute our infrastructure strategy," and "securing energy resources" — the disclosure groundwork for the C2H26 bond debut JPM expects. Demand cover: **commercial RPO $678B, +84% y/y** (total $684B; +25% ex-OpenAI per the call) — ⚠️ CORRECTED 07-30: the duration read is **shorter, not longer**: weighted-avg duration **2.5 yrs (Dec-25) → 2.5 yrs (Mar-26) → 2.3 yrs (Jun-26)** and commercial next-12M share **25% → 25% → 30%** (the "longer-dated vs Q1's 40%" framing held only against the pre-$250B-commitment Q1 mix; the Q2/Q3 10-Qs refute it). Net PP&E **$313.1B** (from $205.0B at Jun-25, +53%); depreciation $34.3B vs $22.0B FY25 — the base the call's 15→25-year useful-life extension operates on. (MSFT FY26 10-K, 2026-07-29) [Filing](../MSFT/MSFT_10-K_2026-07-29_0001193125-26-323660.html)
- **Usage-based pricing inflection + multi-model (MSFT IR, 2026-05-18; UBS 2026-04-30):** a third AI-monetization vector beyond Azure and Copilot seats — the shift from pure per-seat to **per-seat + consumption** (live in GitHub Copilot, "TAM-expansive over the medium term") extending to knowledge-work and security; UBS later flags M365 as a candidate to make this transition seamlessly (sticky, high-usage), with MSFT rolling out additional usage-based pricing in mid-June (UBS/Keirstead, 2026-06-17). On models, MSFT is explicitly **multi-model**: Foundry customers using both Anthropic + OpenAI models up **2x q/q (10,000+ using >1 model)**; new Copilot agents Critique (an Anthropic model auditing an OpenAI model), Council, and Auto mode; OpenAI IP is royalty-free (just COGS) vs a commercial agreement for Anthropic (MSFT IR, 2026-05-18). [UBS tokenmaxxing](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_06_18_karl_ubs_18_jun_26.html)

- **★ 4QFY26 PRINT + CALL — the quarter beat on the two things the page said mattered (Azure and the guide), and the headline EPS needs unpacking before it is used (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29; FY ended 2026-06-30):** **FY26 closes at revenue >$331B, +18%, with operating income +21% to >$155B, Microsoft Cloud >$214B (+27%) and Azure through $100B for the first time (+41%)** — Hood: _"This fiscal year, we delivered over $331 billion in revenue with growth accelerating to 18%… Operating income growth outpaced revenue growth, increasing 21% to more than $155 billion."_ **Q4: revenue $90B, +18% (+17% cc); GM dollars +15%; operating income +18%; company GM 67% (vs the 66.2% guide and UBS's 67.7% prior); opex +10%; operating margin 45%, up slightly y/y (vs the 44.0% guide); total company headcount −2% y/y; OCF $55.4B, +30%; FCF $19.6B; $10.2B returned in the quarter, >$43B for FY26.** **EPS $4.74, +23% "when adjusted for the impact from our investment in OpenAI"** — but **$0.27 of that is discrete vs the April guide**: _"Several discrete items impacted our financial results in the quarter when compared to our forward-looking guidance provided on our April earnings call, resulting in a benefit of $0.27 on diluted earnings per share. These included a **$3.2 billion gain from our investment in Anthropic** and lower-than-expected expenses related to the voluntary retirement program, which were partially offset by severance expense and impairment charges in Xbox."_ **So the clean number is ≈$4.47 vs UBS's $4.41 and consensus $4.24 — a real beat, but roughly a third the size the headline implies**, and Hood is explicit that _"when adjusting for these items, we exceeded expectations across revenue, operating income, and earnings per share."_ **OI&E was $2.8B "when adjusted for the impact of our investments in OpenAI," driven by the Anthropic gain** — i.e. the OpenAI equity-method drag is still being carved out of the line, and what is left inside it this quarter is a private-mark gain, not operating income. **Azure grew 43%** against the 39-40% cc guide and the buyside's 40.7-41% bogey (the call gives Azure without a cc split; Intelligent Cloud cc ran 1pt below reported, so cc ≈42%) — **Intelligent Cloud $39.3B, +32% (+31% cc), segment GM$ +24%, OI +31%, OM 41% (flat y/y)**; on-premises server ~flat (−1% cc), ahead of expectations on renewal in-period rev-rec. Hood's attribution is efficiency, exactly as the page has carried it since April: growth was _"ahead of expectations driven by efficiency gains across our CPU and GPU fleet as well as process improvements to enable earlier delivery of new capacity. That additional in-quarter capacity for Azure was quickly monetized"_ — plus _"stronger-than-expected GitHub co-pilot consumption following the June business model change."_ **Microsoft Cloud revenue $59.3B, +27%, with GM 65% — better than the ~64% guide** (UBS was at 64%). **Commercial bookings +18% ex-OpenAI; +10% (+11% cc) including OpenAI Azure commitments. Commercial RPO $678B, +84% y/y, +25% ex-OpenAI, and the mix disclosure is the material one: _"All sequential commercial RPO growth was driven by commitments from customers outside of Frontier model companies"_** — weighted-average duration 2.3 yrs, ~30% recognizing in the next 12 months (that slice +37% y/y) and the **beyond-12-month portion +112%**. Same point on the revenue base: FY26 cloud revenue >$214B **"with nearly 90% from customers outside of Frontier model companies"** — the cleanest management answer yet to the ~45%-of-RPO OpenAI-concentration bear on this page. **PBP $37.8B, +14%** (M365 Commercial Cloud +16% adjusted / +14% reported; **M365 Commercial products +19%, ahead**, on long-duration contracts with Windows commercial on-prem in-period rev-rec; paid M365 commercial seats +6% y/y, adds skewed to SMB/frontline; consumer cloud +24% (+22% cc), consumer subs +7%; LinkedIn +12% (+10% cc); Dynamics 365 +13% (+12% cc) with **ERP bookings healthy and CRM continuing to moderate on longer sales cycles**; segment OM 58%). **MPC $12.9B, −4% (−5% cc)** — Windows OEM & devices −7% / Windows OEM −5% but **ahead of expectations because "OEM and channel partners continued to build inventory given increasing component prices"** (a pull-forward that the FY27 guide then pays for); Search ex-TAC +10% (+9% cc); Xbox −10% (−11% cc), Xbox content & services −10%, with impairment charges in Xbox in opex; segment OM 21%. (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29) [Transcript](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/Microsoft_Corp_Earnings_Call_2026729_DN000000003114409501pdf.html)
- **⚠ CAPEX BASIS RECONCILIATION — the call's $41B and this page's computed ~$42.7B are on the SAME (lease-inclusive) basis, so the ~$1.3-1.7B gap is a build-up artifact, not a gross/net difference. Both numbers stay on the page, labelled (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29 vs MSFT FY26 10-K, 2026-07-29):** the call states _"**Capital expenditures were $41 billion, including the impact from higher component pricing as noted in our guide. Roughly two-thirds of our CapEx was for short-lived assets, primarily CPUs and GPUs** as customers increasingly build solutions that leverage both AI and non-AI infrastructure. The remaining spend was for long-lived assets. **This quarter, total finance leases were $5.6 billion** and were primarily for large data center sites. And **cash paid for PP&E was $35.8 billion**."_ **$35.8B + $5.6B = $41.4B → the $41B IS lease-inclusive (all-in), confirmed off management's own component split** — the same conceptual basis as the 10-K-derived figure, so this is **not** the gross/net-basis artifact the UBS-$261B-vs-BofA-$243.5B spread on this page is. **The 10-K plug of ~$42.7B was computed by subtraction** — FY26 all-in ≈$140.6B ($115.9B cash additions to P&E + $24.6B finance-lease ROU assets obtained) **less** the three reported prior quarters ($28.5B + $37.5B + $31.9B = $97.9B) — and the residual **~$1.3B (vs the $41.4B components) to ~$1.7B (vs the rounded $41B)** is most plausibly (a) the annual ASC-842 supplemental "**ROU assets obtained in exchange for finance-lease liabilities**" being a broader measure than the sum of the four quarters' "total finance leases" management quotes on calls (Q4: $5.6B), and (b) rounding in each quarterly figure. **Resolution carried on the page: use $41B as the reported 4QFY26 all-in capex (primary, management-stated, component-verified); the ~$42.7B stays flagged as a COMPUTED plug from annual 10-K lines, not a disclosure.** Cross-check against the marks already here: UBS modelled **4Q/Jun $41.8B** (07-26) — essentially on the number; the guide was ">$40B". (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29; MSFT FY26 10-K, 2026-07-29)
- **★ THE CAPEX HEADLINE IS AN ACCOUNTING RECLASSIFICATION, NOT A SPENDING CUT — CY26 ~$190B → ~$175B, and Q1FY27 capex steps to >$50B (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29). THESIS-DRIFT: the ~$190B CY26 guide (Q3 FY26, 2026-04-29) is superseded on a reclassified basis — old value moved to the Changelog.** Hood: _"effective at the start of FY27, we are **extending the estimated useful life of our data centers and office buildings from 15 to 25 years**, reflecting our operating history and expected use of these assets… This change affects only the timing of future depreciation and is expected to have a **minimal benefit to FY27 operating income**. **The greater impact is on capital expenditures as more of our future data center leases will shift from finance leases to operating leases** as a result of this update. **Finance leases are included in capital expenditures, while operating leases are not.** Outside of this useful life impact, **our calendar year 2026 CapEx investment expectations remain unchanged. However, the shift from finance to operating leases adjusts our expectation to approximately $175 billion.**"_ **Read this carefully before marking any capex bogey to it: the ~$15B reduction is a change in what lands inside the reported capex line, not a change in committed spend or in economic obligation — and it makes MSFT's capex line LESS comparable to peers and to the buyside bogeys on this page ($243.5B BofA / $255-260B UBS / ~$260B JPM for FY27, all struck on the old definition).** It also cuts the other way on the depreciation debate already on this page: the **15→25-year useful-life extension is exactly the "hyperscalers flatter earnings by extending useful life" charge** logged under Risks (Burry-style), now taken on buildings/DC shells rather than IT equipment — and management pre-empts it by calling the FY27 operating-income benefit "minimal." **FY27 guidance:** another year of **double-digit revenue and operating income growth**; opex growth **mid-to-high single digits**; **capex will grow y/y "given demand signals across our portfolio"**; full-year **operating margins down less than a point** (vs UBS's expected "down modestly, 50-100bps" — in line); **FCF positive in FY27**; tax rate ~20%; FX <1pt drag. Headwinds named for the full year: M365 commercial products AND server products both **−mid-single digits** on lapping transactional purchasing, and **Windows OEM & devices −high-teens** (higher component costs raising device prices + the Win10 comp + elevated channel inventory). **Q1FY27 guide: total revenue $89.85-90.95B (+16-17%); COGS $29.6-29.8B (+23-24%); opex $16.8-16.9B (+7-8%); operating margins ~flat y/y; OI&E ex-OpenAI ≈ −$100M (interest expense incl. DC finance-lease interest more than offsetting interest income); capex "over $50 billion, including the lease reclassification impact from the useful life update."** Segments: PBP $36.7-37.0B (+11-12%), M365 CC ~+16% cc adjusted / 15% reported with **acceleration in commercial cloud growth expected through the fiscal year** on E5/E7 + July usage-based billing; Intelligent Cloud $40.95-41.25B (+33-34%) with **Azure ~+45% cc** and _"we continue to expect H1 growth to accelerate"_; MPC $12.2-12.7B with **Windows OEM & devices −low 20s** and the range "wider than normal." **The Azure guide is the number that breaks the setup on this page: ~45% cc vs a buyside 1QFY27 bogey of 41.4% (BofA) / 42% (UBS) — a ~3pt raise against the bar, not a reaffirm.** (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29)
- **★ The MAI/multi-model leg went from a slide to a P&L argument, and it is now pointed at MSFT's own suppliers (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29):** the catalog is **"the broadest model catalog in the cloud with over 11,000 models, including the latest from OpenAI, Anthropic, Mistral, X-AI as well as our own MAI family,"** with **a 5x increase since the start of the year in the number of customers building with models from multiple providers** and **Levi Strauss & Co running OpenAI *and* Anthropic models on Foundry across "more than 1,000 domain-specific agents"** — the successor datapoint to the "10,000+ Foundry customers using >1 model, up 2x q/q" already on this page (MSFT IR, 05-18). **What is new is that MAI is now displacing frontier tokens inside Microsoft's own flagship products, with quantified savings:** **MAI Code 1 Flash on GitHub Copilot** used by _"millions of developers… achieving **higher code acceptance rates and 10% lower median token usage** while still having access to Frontier capabilities from OpenAI and Anthropic"_; **in Excel, MAI Code 1 Flash is _"delivering comparable quality to GPT56 [GPT-5.6] for the most common task while operating at significantly lower costs"_**; **MAI Cyber 1 Flash** _"achieves better performance than much larger Mithos [Anthropic Mythos] model, but at half the cost when combined with our multi-agent security harness"_ — Satya later quantifies the mechanism: _"we proved with the MDASH data in Cybergym that essentially you can have those level performance with **50% less cost**… because **90% of the tasks are done by the Cyber Flash One model and 10% of the tasks you still go to the frontier**."_ Plus **89% reduction of GPU costs in Dynamics 365 with MAI Voice 2 Flash and up to 84% reduced GPU costs in PowerPoint with MAI Image 25**, a dozen-plus new models including **MAI Thinking One (first MSFT reasoning model)**, and **40% better performance per watt running MAI models on Maia 200**. The architectural framing is the durable part and it is the same one Octahedron flagged (07-15): _"we are building a new model system where the **harness, context, memory, and action space are separate from any one model family**, thereby moving the frontier on the cost-to-outcome curve… **every model is substitutable**"_ — and Satya explicitly ties it to resilience: _"if you look even at the **hugging face incident**… you can't sort of depend on any one model… **you can't be subject to the refusals of one model**."_ **Read for the bear case on this page (SemiAnalysis 07-09 "completely blown their early lead"; JPM 07-23 "No Model, No Chip"): MSFT's answer is not a frontier model of its own, it is a cheap-model-plus-harness cost structure that it is willing to point at GPT and Claude — which is bullish MSFT's margin and bearish the frontier labs' share of MSFT's own token spend.** Related first-party silicon: **Maia 200 "continues to scale… 30% better performance per dollar than the latest generation hardware in our fleet and is now supporting both OpenAI and MAI models"** (the first time the page has Maia carrying OpenAI workloads), **Cobalt 200 racks in over 25 data centers by end of month**, and MSFT to be **"among the first cloud providers to deploy next-generation scale AI infrastructure based on AMD Helios and NVIDIA Vera Rubin."** (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29)
- **Capacity, product and Copilot marks from the call worth carrying (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29):** **31 new data centers this quarter across five continents, 88 for the year; another gigawatt of capacity added this quarter and "on track to roughly double our overall capacity in just two years"; dock-to-live times for new GPUs in the largest regions reduced by nearly 50% over the fiscal year; Copilot workload throughput up 4x since the start of the year.** Hood on the constraint, unchanged and now with a market-price tell: _"there are still constraints in the system… demand continues to exceed available supply. And that certainly remains true. **You can even see it, I think in some of the pricing that's occurring in the spot market for assets.**"_ **M365 Copilot: over 30 million paid seats with net seat adds more than doubling q/q** — vs >20M at Q3 FY26, the 25M+ guide UBS carried, and FUNDA's 37.0mn channel model (07-27), i.e. **the print lands between the guide and the most aggressive channel estimate**; customers with >50,000 seats **+7x y/y**; enterprises deploying Copilot to a majority of information workers **+~75% q/q**; time-from-deployment-to-high-usage down "from months to just days"; user-satisfaction scores doubled over three quarters, **latency cut 25% this quarter**; conversations per user nearly doubled y/y with weekly engagement "on par with Outlook and teams." Named deployments: **NHS England 505,000 clinicians/staff** (trial showed 43 minutes/day saved), **KPMG >276,000**, **HSBC 200,000**, and AstraZeneca / Boeing / Infosys / Coke / P&G / Stellantis / TCS / UPMC / Wells Fargo each **60,000+**. **E7 two months post-launch: "hundreds of enterprise customers have already purchased millions of seats," and EY deployed E7 to 400,000 employees ("our largest win to date")** — which sits against the UBS field work on this page that E7 is being bought on 10-50% discounts with minimal ARPU uplift; both stay on the page. **GitHub Copilot: 50 million users; "Copilot revenue accelerated over 60% quarter-over-quarter"** after the June usage-based-billing change (the on-page UBS raise to FY27 $1.95bn now has a company-level growth rate behind it); all of GitHub **225 million users**, >90% of the F500, and **one in three pull requests on GitHub now involves an agent**. Platform: **100,000 Foundry customers with revenue more than doubled y/y**, Foundry customers at a **1-trillion-token annualized run rate +4x y/y**, **Agent 365 with nearly 40 million agents registered across tens of thousands of companies two months in** (vs FUNDA's "will not be fast" 07-27 caution — both stay), **40,000+ paid Fabric customers (+>60% y/y)**, PostgreSQL revenue **+55%, accelerating a third consecutive quarter**, and **Cowork GA last month with usage-based billing added earlier this month ("thousands of customers already paying for and actively using it")**. Also: **Mistral partnership for Microsoft Sovereign Cloud** announced the prior week; **Project Perception** (agentic security) to be sold on a consumption basis; **Microsoft Frontier Co** — 6,000 embedded industry/engineering experts, built off 330+ projects across 164 customers. **Xbox: management "expect[s] to return the business to growth in fiscal 2027"** after the reset (impairments + studio sales already on the page). (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29)
- **How management answered the two bear questions on the call — the overbuild hedge and the component-cost pass-through (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29, Mark Moerdler/Bernstein Q):** on overcapacity risk, Hood makes the short-lived-asset mix the hedge: _"you've seen our CapEx really pivot toward what I would call and do call **short-lived assets**, which really… that's CPUs and GPUs that have **relatively shorter lead times**. And so **if the demand environment changes, you just slow down what is in fact the largest component**… The investment into land and data center builds is actually quite flexible… a **smaller percentage of the overall cost structure**… you have the ability to **late bind some of the more expensive components in short term**."_ On component inflation: _"that's really impacting everybody equivalently… the cloud offers tremendous benefits versus having to make these purchases as servers on-prem yourself where the **price increases are even more hard for customers**… a lot of this obviously is also being sold in **newer contracts and we're able to have the pricing reflected**"_ — i.e. pass-through via new-contract pricing, consistent with the Intel-V7-SKU-priced-above-V6 lever from the 06-08 IR call. Satya's macro framing is the quotable one and is notably cycle-aware: _"**All of us are reading this 1873 as the book to be read**… you've got to get the product shape right… the portfolio right… the mix of customers… and then you got to run an efficient railroad… we know that there will be **ups and downs of what is the cycle here, but the secular shift is clear**."_ On ROI (Gabriela Borges/GS Q), Hood declines to claim the math improved — _"I don't know that quite frankly, my math has changed in terms of how I do it over the past year"_ — and instead lists levers: TAM expansion, product + infrastructure efficiency, first-party silicon price/performance, and **model diversification as an explicit margin lever** (_"being able to serve the best possible outcome with a more efficient… token usage and efficient in terms of cost structure are also margin levers"_), closing on _"**this is the grind work**."_ Note what is absent: **no FY27 capex dollar figure, no CY27 number, and no update to the ~$20B component pre-buy disclosed in the 10-K** — the two open questions the page flagged into the print stay open. (MSFT 4QFY26 earnings call, 2026-07-29)

## Debate / thesis
### 🆕🔴🔴 2026-09-15 (/wiki-ingest, 21:00) — **MICROSOFT JOINED THE "PACING" CAMP WITH A PRODUCT COMMITMENT RATHER THAN A POSITION PAPER — KILL SWITCHES AND SHUTDOWN RULES — AND EVERY DESK IMMEDIATELY RE-READ IT AS A DISTRIBUTION ARGUMENT: IF ENTERPRISES SLOW DOWN, THE "SAFE" DEPLOYMENT VENDOR WINS THE WORKLOAD**

- 🔴🔴 **THE EVENT.** *"Microsoft Joins Calls for AI 'Pacing,' Pledges to Build Kill Switches Into AI Products"* (**The Information**, 2026-09-15, relayed the same morning by the **UBS tech daily**); CNBC framed it as *"Microsoft sets limits for future AI models as industry throttles frontier development"* (2026-09-14), and the policy read-through arrived within the day: *"South Korea updates AI safeguards as Microsoft sets shutdown rules"* (Digitimes, 2026-09-15). ➤ **This is a different act from Amodei's essay and should not be filed with it: Anthropic proposed a coordination REGIME; Microsoft shipped a PRODUCT FEATURE and a vendor policy. The first is unenforceable by a single firm; the second is a procurement checkbox that a regulator can point at — which is precisely why Seoul cited it inside 24 hours.**
- 🔴 **NADELLA, ON STAGE, SAME 48 HOURS (All-In Summit, 2026-09-14/15; relayed by the Morgan Stanley TMT desk, 2026-09-15 ⚠️ *sales commentary, not research*).** He *"said we should always endeavor to have humans in control with competent devops to monitor. Yes to third party testing."* Two further points that are product roadmap rather than politics: he *"already sees massive model capability overhang — models are great already, but we haven't made enough use of what we have and still need to drive more business / consumer use"*, and he *"wants KV cache standards so that you can share it among many different AI model families operating together."* ➤➤ **THE CAPABILITY-OVERHANG LINE IS THE MOST COMMERCIALLY LOADED THING HE SAID: it is the CEO of the largest reseller of frontier capability arguing that the binding constraint on AI revenue is DIFFUSION, not model quality — i.e. a pause at the frontier costs Microsoft's P&L very little, which is exactly the position from which endorsing "pacing" is cheap.** The KV-cache standardisation ask points the other way and is genuinely new for this page: it is an argument for multi-model serving economics (and a direct cost lever on inference), cross-ref [[themes/tokenmaxxing]].
- 🔭 **THE DESK READ, AND IT IS THE BULL CASE IN ONE SENTENCE: *"MAYBE Enterprises slow down given concerns of Agents going rogue, which should arguably benefit a MSFT type as the 'safe' AI deployment option. Satya Nadella has been talking about Security as a competitive differentiator for two years but nobody cared until now"*** (**Morgan Stanley TMT desk · Tom Wigg**, 2026-09-15). Corroborating from a second desk, on the enterprise data-retention backlash against the labs: *"**Microsoft and Salesforce push their own isolated environments as a privacy buffer**"*, with marquee customers ([[PLTR]], [[NVDA]], Booz Allen, Northrop, a US utility) restricting the frontier models over data-retention and IP concerns (**Rothschild & Co TMT Daily · Rowan Adley**, 2026-09-15 ⚠️ *specialist sales*, summarising The Information's 2026-09-14 reporting). ⚠️ One FinTwit claim adds the sharper version — *"Microsoft, which owns 25% of OpenAI, is using these fears to lure OpenAI customers to its own AI solutions"* (**@Hesamation**, 2026-09-14, 44k views ⚠️ *not a broker; the 25% stake figure is the poster's claim and is NOT verified here*). ➤ **File this as the cleanest statement yet of the hedge embedded in this name: Microsoft monetises the frontier when it accelerates and monetises the governance layer when it does not.** Cross-ref [[themes/ai-cybersecurity]] (2026-09-15) and [[ANTHROPIC]] / [[OPENAI]] for the customer-side evidence.
- ⚠️ **THE FUNDING FACT THAT SITS UNDER ALL OF IT, AND IT IS THE ONE FORWARD TEST ON THIS PAGE NOBODY IS WATCHING: of the big four, Microsoft is the ONLY one yet to issue — BofA's new hyperscaler-credit initiation models the group having issued $278bn to date, with further issuance of $310bn (2026), $215bn (2027) and $125bn (2028), and puts [[GOOG]]/[[AMZN]] 45-50% through their need, [[META]] ~30%, and MSFT at zero with $75bn needed through YE28** (**BofA Global Research · Tom Curcuruto**, 2026-09-15, initiating Marketweight on MSFT credit and Overweight on GOOG ⚠️ *relayed via the BofA TMT desk*). ⚠️ **BASIS GUARD: issuance, not capex** — do not net against the capex series on [[themes/hyperscaler-capex]]. ➤ *A first-time issuer with $75bn to place inside 27 months is a dated, falsifiable event path; log each deal here as it prices.*

- 🆕🔴 **THE PRIMARY TRANSCRIPT LANDED (Nadella on stage at the All-In Summit, interviewed by the four All-In Podcast hosts, recorded 2026-09-14 / aired 2026-09-15 — full transcript archived [MSFT_All-In-Summit-interview_2026-09-15.md](../MSFT/transcripts/MSFT_All-In-Summit-interview_2026-09-15.md)) — AND IT CARRIES FOUR THINGS THE MS-DESK RELAY ABOVE DIDN'T.** (i) **Agentic risk goes beyond "humans in control": he frames a long-running agent inside an enterprise as a *new class of insider risk* — his own example, an agent told to "go optimize my working capital" that could "fake my books" — with the fix framed as product engineering (aggressive behavioral monitoring/audit trails on every object an agent touches) plus a standing chain-of-thought-transparency commitment ("making sure the CoTs are in language that we can all understand... transparent"), the same mechanism underpinning the kill-switch/shutdown-rule commitment logged above.** (ii) **On the harness-not-model thesis this page already carries, his prescribed enterprise architecture is an explicit sovereignty test: *"pull out a model and see whether you can retain the eval — if you can't, you really are dependent on something that may not be yours"* — "use all, but be independent of all," a procurement standard, not just a Microsoft feature; cross-ref the KV-cache-standardisation ask already on the page.** (iii) **Pressed on why enterprises pay ~$50/M tokens when open alternatives price near ~$0.60/M, Nadella's answer is "good old-fashioned competition" — the open-source check on closed models is structurally the same dynamic that let Postgres/MySQL cap Windows/SQL-Server pricing; model companies "will do fine" managing token pricing by family, but the app tier only becomes economically viable "with a margin" because of that check — the CEO's own version of the harness/app-layer-captures-the-margin bull case already dominant on this page.** (iv) **Capex mechanics in his own words: two asset classes — long-duration ("land, power, cold shell") vs. short-duration **"kit" (racks/chips, ~60% of the cost)** — built where possible, leased for scale, **rented when a surge needs covering** ("we're even renting quite a bit... because we were short on supply"); explicit non-concentration intent — *"it's great that OpenAI is one of our largest customers... but we need more [customers], I don't want to build for two or three"*; silicon stays heterogeneous by design — Nvidia "our primary thing," OpenAI building its own chip, AMD "in there" — goal is running OpenAI/Anthropic/MSFT's own models on the same heterogeneous kit, not a single-vendor bet.**
- 🆕 **THE SAME INTERVIEW PUTS A CEO-STATED DENOMINATOR ON THE COPILOT TAM DEBATE, AND A NAMED COUNTER-ANECDOTE ON THE PERMITTING BACKLASH.** Nadella's own sizing: of the ~450M total Microsoft 365 knowledge-worker seats (incl. students), he narrows the real addressable market to **"maybe 300... 250 even" million "real enterprise users,"** against which the **~30M paid Copilot seats already on this page (Q4'26 table) reads as ~10-12% of his own stated addressable base**, a different penetration read than JPM's "~7% of a 450mn+ commercial seat base" already on file — flag both denominators, do not average. **On the social-license/permitting backlash this page has tracked since 09-05 ([[themes/ai-datacenter-power]], not yet folded into that theme page), he offers a named counter-anecdote: Microsoft's Quincy, WA campus (started ~2008, now "at least 4 or 500 megawatts" and still expanding) — tax revenue **"up 12 times,"** paid-in taxes **"down by a third,"** town growth outpacing Seattle, a new school/hospital/town-center/aquatic-center, and **~1,200 sustained construction jobs across the 20-year build** (not a one-time number) — offered explicitly as the "earn permission" story he wants told by people outside tech, not by Microsoft itself.** ⚠️ **Single-site, management-selected anecdote — not audited, not representative of the contested sites already on the theme page (recall votes, >70% national poll opposition); log as MSFT's own chosen counter-example, not as resolving the backlash debate.** Separately, his macro bogey for the whole AI thesis: **"at least 7-8% GDP growth that is real and... broad-based"** — without it, in his own industrial-revolution framing, productivity gains just compress hours worked rather than showing up as growth, a management-articulated version of the diffusion-not-capability argument already logged above.

- **Street split — Ackman "core holding" vs. Hohn/TCI's $8B exit (both dated 2026-05-15/16):** the cleanest two-sided read on MSFT positioning this quarter, per Barron's "Up & Down Wall Street" (Andy Serwer, 2026-05-18 issue). **Bull side:** Bill Ackman disclosed via an 887-word tweet on **Friday, 2026-05-15** that he had acquired a MSFT position and called it **"a core holding."** **Bear side:** Chris Hohn (TCI Fund Management) sold **most of an $8 billion stake held for 10 years**; his shareholder letter: **"We reduced our investment in Microsoft because the rapid progress in AI introduces uncertainty over Microsoft's competitive position in the future."** Same print, same week, diametrically opposed conclusions from two large, credible allocators — Ackman betting on MSFT's AI-stack breadth/monetization, Hohn exiting on AI-competitive-position uncertainty (the same "custom silicon/models structural weak spot" and OpenAI-dependency themes elaborated below). Context on the print itself: Q3 FY26 adjusted EPS **$4.27 vs $4.05 est.** on revenue **$82.9B vs $81.4B est.**; Azure grew **40%** (Barron's/Serwer, 2026-05-18).
- **Bull:** Bernstein reiterates **Outperform, TP $646**, calling MSFT strongly positioned in an AI-bubble case — first-party capacity usage ramping via a diversified app base, third-party contracts beyond OpenAI, and contract duration well-aligned with equipment useful life; Azure stable→accelerating with GAAP margins in the mid-40s, and current valuation an "attractive entry point" (Bernstein "Cloud in the Quarter", 2026-06-22). Bernstein estimates Azure GAAP gross margin ~56.4% in 3FQ26 (vs ~61.5% y/y) and frames AI-vs-non-AI margins converging as MSFT manages opex to flat FY26 op margin (Bernstein "Cloud in the Quarter", 2026-06-22). AI is a real, large engine, not eating SaaS — Copilot is sold separately, not bundled/discounted, seat growth accelerating into E7 launch (Bernstein hyperscaler review, 2026-04-30). Margins expanded despite record capex; Bernstein's read is this "rerates" exactly like the prior cloud transition — opex grows slower than revenue, and capex eventually grows slower than cloud revenue, inflecting AI/Azure margins up (Bernstein, 2026-04-30). First-party silicon (Maia 200 >30% better tokens/$, Cobalt in ~half of regions) and first-party MAI models lower COGS; FinTwit flags MSFT at ~16.8x 2028E as "stupid cheap," targeting ~50% of AI compute on own silicon by 2028 (@ParadisLabs, 2026-06-15). Ackman built a new core MSFT position at ~21x fwd, M365+Azure ~70% of profits (13F, 2026-05-15/18; called it "a core holding" per his 2026-05-15 tweet — see Street-split item above). GS a "Buyer" post-Build (Borges, 2026-06-07). MS reiterates **OW $482**, durable mid-teens EPS CAGR, and sees the GAAP PEG gap (1.7x ex-OpenAI loss vs ~1.9x large-cap avg on CY27) as closeable — plus any OpenAI equity value as free upside (MS MSFT/OpenAI, 2025-05-21). **Judson Althoff** (new commercial-side chief, took over Oct. 2025) directly rebuts the capex/competition bears: **"They don't concern me, because I think the market is still trying to figure out AI... it comes from the place of seeing parlor tricks... Yes, it's expensive [capex]. I don't get concerned. We're building for our first-party AI solutions and infrastructure for Anthropic..."** — framing the capex as demand-backed, not speculative (Barron's/Serwer, 2026-05-18). **Tigress Financial Partners: Buy, PT $680** (stock then $407); Copilot "adoption is scaling rapidly, with paid Copilot seats growing triple digits YoY to tens of millions of users" out of ~450M total business seats (Barron's/Serwer, 2026-05-18) — corroborates the >20M paid-Copilot-seat figure already on file.
- **Bear:** Capex is the battleground — MS/Altimeter revised CY27 capex est. from $178B to **$276B**, the single biggest hyperscaler revision (@Midnight_Captl, 2026-06-13); 1-yr return ~ -18% with the "lowest ROI on AI capex to-date" critique (@ParadisLabs, 2026-06-12). SemiAnalysis quantifies the ROIC problem: MSFT's own AI ROIC is "not much higher than ORCL's ~20%" once you strip the OpenAI rev-share (set to end 2030–2032), vs 35–40% blended MSFT today (SemiAnalysis MSFT AI Strategy, 2025-11-12). The "Big Pause" (CY24 datacenter freeze) ceded share: MSFT paused >3.5GW that would have been built by 2028, dropped out of multi-GW of LOIs (Phoenix, Chicago, UK, Nordics, Japan, India, LatAm) that went to ORCL/Meta/CRWV/Google, and lost the $100B Stargate contract to Oracle — effectively stepping away from ~$150B of OpenAI gross-profit dollars (~$30B/yr, which would have added >18% to FY25's $194B GP) (SemiAnalysis, 2025-11-12). Now back in the market but "running out of options," MSFT is renting GPUs from neoclouds and reselling — structurally lower Azure margin (SemiAnalysis, 2025-11-12). UBS adds the disclosure nuance: the ORCL/CRWV subcontractor workloads report as gross Azure revenue (their revs = MSFT COGS), and the biggest medium-term Azure risk is whether OpenAI *inference* (not just training) migrates off Azure (UBS, 2025-09-02). OpenAI concentration in Azure backlog (~45% RPO; TCI flagged single customer >40% of Azure backlog before cutting its $8B stake, 2026-05-11 — see the Hohn shareholder-letter quote in the Street-split item above). DeepSeek/"tokenmaxxing" substitution risk — MSFT reportedly weighing hosted DeepSeek V4 + usage pricing on Copilot Cowork (Axios via briefing, 2026-06-17; @zerohedge, 2026-06-18). Stratechery's read sharpens the strategic logic: under usage-based pricing (E7 bundles Anthropic+OpenAI for $99/employee/month), MSFT is incentivized to offer a "significantly higher level of usage" via a cheap open-source model rather than ceding margin to Anthropic/OpenAI on every token — MSFT confirmed to Axios it is exploring a fine-tuned DeepSeek V4 (or another open-source model) as a lower-cost alternative powering Copilot Cowork, expecting to make it available "in the coming weeks"; Thompson expects Cowork best-with-Claude and Copilot best-with-GPT, with DeepSeek the bundle's cost-efficient default, and thinks MSFT goes this route — framing American software cos as the biggest beneficiaries of a cheaper Chinese model (Stratechery, 2026-06-23). Barclays desk: investors see MSFT "stuck in the mud" near-term, used as a funding long vs SaaS, with Azure growth decelerating relative to GCP/AWS accelerating (Rand, 2026-06-03); positioning moved from +2 to -1 post-Q2 (Barclays, 2026-05-29). HE flags "Growing MSFT Risk" thematically (HE Global Technology BB, 2025-12-04). Sell-side spec-sales desks corroborate the positioning bear: BofA called MSFT the **"most hated Mag7," "dead for sale"** in the long-only community (reflexive selling as the underweight grows), caught in the "software is going to die" thesis — the core gripe being that an Anthropic Excel plugin works better than native Copilot despite MSFT's head start, and that Azure is capped near-term because GPUs are diverted to internal R&D rather than chasing a mid-40s Azure number (BofA/Wigg, 2026-05-06; BofA/Fenske, 2026-04-16). A Jefferies CIO check echoed it: enthusiasm "tempered not on Azure but on applications" (Power Apps vs custom LLM/Snowflake/Databricks builds), with ~5 cases of ~$5M each that may not renew at SaaS vendors (Jefferies conf, 2026-06-01); a cloud-MSP expert ranked **Google — not AWS — as MSFT's biggest competitive threat** (most "resilient" post-outages, not a competitor in many verticals) and said Copilot was "sold the wrong way" as a silver bullet (up to 96% adoption when positioned as an assistant, but loses to Claude Code/Cursor on coding) (WFC cloud/MSFT expert, 2026-04-14). Into the print, the Azure bogey was Street 37.5% / buy-side ~38.4%, needing 38–40% for the stock to work, with Copilot at 15M seats = only ~3.5% of Office attach (BofA/Fenske preview, 2026-04-20). **By the 7/12 bogey poll the bar has ratcheted up materially — buyside now carries a 4QFY26 Azure cc bogey of 40.7% and a 1QFY27 guide bogey of 41.4% (vs ~38.4% in April), i.e. the number MSFT must clear to work the stock is ~2pts higher into the 7/29 print; buyside capex bogeys also stepped up to FY27 $243.5B / FY28 $276.9B / FY29 $289.0B (with Big-5 '26-28 capex intensity moving 37.5% → ~46.5% on buyside bogeys)** (BofA · Brian Fenske (buyside bogey poll), 2026-07-12). [Wigg](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_05_06_wigg_6_may_26.html) · [Fenske](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_04_16_fenske_16_apr_26.html) · [Jefferies conf](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_06_01_brent_jeff_ai_1_jun_26.html) · [WFC cloud expert](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_04_14_wfc_cloud_msft_14_apr_2026.html) · [BofA preview](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_04_20_fenske_20_apr_26.html)
- **Bear (independent, harsh, attributed) — "Microsoft has completely blown their early lead with GitHub Copilot" (SemiAnalysis "The Future of Meta Superintelligence: A 1 Year Progress Update", 2026-07-09):** in a piece arguing that frontier AI "has increasingly felt like **a two horse race between OpenAI vs Anthropic**," SemiAnalysis delivers the bluntest competitive verdict on MSFT now on this page: "**Microsoft has completely blown their early lead with GitHub copilot and failed to effectively leverage their access to OpenAI IP.**" Context for how the field is being scored in the same paragraph: Google "faded dramatically" after Gemini 3 Pro/Nano Banana (3.5 Flash "a benchmaxxed prop" that performs far worse than GPT 5.6 and Opus 4.8 in the real world; 3.5 Pro "not even Opus level on coding"), and the Chinese labs are "simply too compute poor to truly reach the frontier." Logged as the independent-analyst opinion it is — no numbers attached — but it is the same charge the sell-side bears make from different angles (GS/Borges 07-08 "Copilot perceived to lag"; JPM/Schilsky 07-23 "No Model, No Chip"; the standing SemiAnalysis 2025-11-12 read that Cursor/Claude Code/Codex out-execute GitHub Copilot), and it directly contests the MSFT-owns-the-OpenAI-IP leg of the bull case. Counterweights already on the page: MS's 2Q26 CIO survey (07-15/07-16) has GitHub Copilot penetration rising **42% → 58%**, and Bernstein (06-22) has GitHub commits running from ~1B to ≥14B. (SemiAnalysis, 2026-07-09) [Source](../relat%C3%B3rios%20bons/2026_07_09_ENC_The_Future_of_Meta_Superintelligence_A_1_Year_Progress_Update_email.html)
